| **[Agentic World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30136v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent world action models provide visual foresight and future prediction for robot manipulation, but lack explicit mechanisms for compositional skill reuse. Agentic robotics combines code-based planning and reasoning with reusable skills, but often lacks predictive grounding in physical dynamics. We introduce AW-0, an agentic world action model framework that integrates code-based planning with joint video and action prediction. We jointly model code, future video, and robot actions as three coupled generative streams within a unified mixture-of-transformers architecture. Within this architecture a code expert generates parameterized skill routines and control logic while video and action experts generate future visual states and low-level motor commands. Code serves two complementary roles: it structures multi-step prediction and control within the world model and represents skills as reusable programs for subsequent agentic execution. We further introduce an automated curation pipeline that labels robot and human video trajectories with aligned programs and consolidates recurring routines into reusable skill libraries. Evaluations on RoboTwin 2.0 and physical bimanual manipulation tasks show improved task performance over VLA and WAM baselines, alongside gains in language following, semantic reasoning and robustness, demonstrating that agentic code enables connection between planning, prediction and execution within WAMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://agentic-wam.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[World of SWECraft: Agent Collaboration at Scale via Simulated Software Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24061v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As teams of AI agents take on larger projects and long-standing open problems, agent collaboration offers another dimension for scaling inference-time compute. To understand what makes such collaboration effective and scalable, we develop Software World, a framework for simulating multi-agent coworking in realistic software ecosystems. It comes with a pipeline to create environments of real, interdependent Python packages of different topics, sizes, and dependency structures, which we call software worlds. In a simulation, agents maintain packages in the world with a high-level goal of improving software efficiency, and they are free to discover tasks to work on and collaborate with others. We evaluate agents' work in a world collectively by measuring speedup in hidden dependent packages. The framework allows us to study different elements of collaboration by varying communication, ownership, and resource allocation. We run simulations in three different worlds, totaling more than 10,000 hours of agent work across eight models. Overall, performance improvement scales with inference-time compute, with the best model improving downstream efficiency by up to 31%. We show that most agents can find meaningful tasks via collaboration, but some frontier models can prematurely stop looking for improvements and make little progress. We release the simulation framework and traces to support future studies of agent collaboration at https://szj.io/sw.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 9 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Quant-Arena: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Quantitative Trading Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30113v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantitative research is an iterative loop: a researcher proposes a market hypothesis, implements it as an executable signal, tests it on historical data, and decides what to keep. Existing benchmarks score formulas, code, or individual trading decisions, leaving open whether large language models (LLMs) can complete this loop and produce strategies that work beyond the research period. We introduce Quant-Arena, a benchmark and public leaderboard for end-to-end quantitative research on real A-share market data. A model constructs features and stock-scoring code, receives feedback only on 2022--2023, and freezes one strategy for assessment on the sealed year 2024. A cash-account evaluator applies trading constraints and costs, including market impact estimated from a simulated order book. Scores are dominated by realized excess return and complemented by signal quality and the diversity of features used by the strategy. Across 64 benchmarks, explicit evaluation centers on Act; only 5 assess Anticipate and 3 assess Verify, leaving both largely hidden behind task success. | **[Privacy Preference Controls for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29929v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In response to vast and widespread online tracking, regulators and technologists alike have worked to develop laws and tools that allow people to express their privacy preferences. | **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.</p></details> | <details><summary>67 pa...</summary><p>67 pages, 14 figures, 26 tables. v2 is a substantial revision with a new title and two added authors. The evaluation is extended from AppWorld to seven agent benchmarks across three decision families (set retrieval, classification, policy and reward), and TabHead is now a CatBoost classifier with an ablation over classical and neural heads</p></details> |
| **[From VLM Planners to Robotic Agents: Self-Evolving Harnesses for History-Dependent Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29560v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models achieve strong performance across embodied manipulation tasks by unifying visual understanding, language conditioning, and action generation. We instantiate this approach as SERA (Self-Evolving Robotic Agent), with a fixed VLA executor providing low-level control. The harness maintains and updates task context using observations and execution feedback to guide subsequent planning. Self-evolution uses environment rollouts to propose and evaluate harness revisions, with the selected configuration fixed before held-out testing. On RoboMME, SERA achieves 51.38% overall success with autonomous selection, compared with 44.38% without self-evolution. Our exploration suggests that agentic planning is a promising direction for history-dependent robotic manipulation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Allocating Indivisible Goods to Strategic Agents: Pure Nash Equilibria and Fairness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.08644v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We consider the problem of fairly allocating a set of indivisible goods to a set of strategic agents with additive valuation functions. We assume no monetary transfers and, therefore, a mechanism in our setting is an algorithm that takes as input the reported -- rather than the true -- values of the agents. Our main goal is to explore whether there exist mechanisms that have pure Nash equilibria for every instance and, at the same time, provide fairness guarantees for the allocations that correspond to these equilibria. We focus on two relaxations of envy-freeness, namely envy-freeness up to one good (EF1), and envy-freeness up to any good (EFX), and we positively answer the above question. In particular, we study two algorithms that are known to produce such allocations in the non-strategic setting: Round-Robin (EF1 allocations for any number of agents) and a cut-and-choose algorithm of Plaut and Roughgarden [SIAM Journal of Discrete Mathematics, 2020] (EFX allocations for two agents). For Round-Robin we show that all of its pure Nash equilibria induce allocations that are EF1 with respect to the underlying true values, while for the algorithm of Plaut and Roughgarden we show that the corresponding allocations not only are EFX but also satisfy maximin share fairness, something that is not true for this algorithm in the non-strategic setting! Further, we show that a weaker version of the latter result holds for any mechanism for two agents that always has pure Nash equilibria which all induce EFX allocations.</p></details> | <details><summary>The c...</summary><p>The claim that every pure Nash equilibrium of Round-Robin is EF1 (Theorem 3.3 here; Theorem 3 in the conference version and Theorem 4 in the journal version) is incorrect; the gap is in the proof of Lemma 3.5 (inequality (2)). A counterexample and corrected guarantees are given in arXiv:2610.23686. The remaining results are not affected</p></details> |
| **[Crystal-structure design by agentic AI in a language of motifs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15900v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data-driven materials discovery interpolates more reliably than it extrapolates and seldom reveals new structure types. We present MatEvolve, an agentic-AI framework that designs crystals in an interpretable \emph{language of motifs}, in which each crystal is written as a \emph{motif profile} that describes the recurring geometric patterns---the \emph{motifs}---composing the crystal. The motif profile serves not merely as a description of a material but as the medium for material design: the agent edits the profile with a stated rationale and constructs a crystal from the modified one; the most promising candidates are then validated by first-principles calculations. MatEvolve is built on the state-of-the-art language model Claude Fable~5 without fine-tuning. It is applied to the design of rare-earth-lean permanent-magnet compounds and finds novel (meta)stable crystals that are both new structural prototypes and magnet compounds; under an equal validation budget, it does so an order of magnitude more often than generative models. Beyond design, analyzing the human-readable profiles of the discovered crystals reveals their structure--property relationships.</p></details> |  |

To disentangle these effects, we introduce FairAMO, a controlled evaluation protocol with frozen candidate forecasts. Experiments on GIFT-Eval show that AMO improves accuracy, but its gains are bounded by the candidate TSFMs. The best orchestrators reduce MASE by less than 6% relative to the best single TSFM across all tasks. These gains depend on complementarity (different candidates excel on different tasks) rather than the number of candidates. Adding members yields diminishing returns, and a redundant member can erase these gains. The value of historical evidence also saturates quickly. A few recent windows capture all gains, and further history adds none. These findings separate system-level forecasting gains from the contribution of orchestration itself.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Foundation Models for Temporal Systems (FMTS)</p></details> |
| **[S$^2$-MAS: A Society-Scale Multi-Agent Simulation Framework with 500 Million Household Persona Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27746v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Simulating economic responses at society scale requires agents whose decisions reflect household circumstances and the effects of earlier actions. Sharp-421m-mlx, a compact decision model trained with SFT + RLCD on 2.93 million examples, achieves 74.55% accuracy and 38.37% question-macro F1 in consumer financial-choice scenarios, outperforming Jev 1.13 and GLM-5.3-Flash on both measures. We evaluate eight financial task families and simulate 12 million distinct household agents in paired purchase-funding conditions. Jointly raising purchase goals and approved credit limits increases consumption, with responses differing across age and income groups. Education, employment and household-size differences persist after age--income standardization and mainly reflect transitions from deferral to purchase. Asset and debt groups differ in cash drawdown, repayment and closing debt. A 24-month panel varies purchase goals under a common credit product, with repayment and arrears updating subsequent offers. S$^2$-MAS links heterogeneous household decisions to evolving consumption, cash and repayment paths, supporting economic experiments across household groups, population scales and time horizons.</p></details> | <details><summary>58 pa...</summary><p>58 pages, 13 main figures, 7 supplementary figures</p></details> |
| **[PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29467v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon dialogue agents need to keep user memories stable enough for personalization while adapting when the user genuinely changes. Existing memory systems often append every observation or overwrite old entries by heuristic rules, which makes transient noise hard to distinguish from real belief drift. We introduce PreCoMem, a memory consolidation framework inspired by predictive coding that represents memory as confidence-weighted beliefs about the user's state. Its core signal, Effective Surprisal, combines semantic deviation and contradiction evidence, then downweights this signal when retrieval is diffuse and unreliable. According to effective surprisal, a two-threshold gate maps each turn to one of three deterministic updates: MAINTAIN reinforces supported beliefs, PROFILE stores ambiguous cues as low-confidence hypotheses, and CORRECT softly decays reliably contradicted facts. The PROFILE stage acts as an explicit waiting room, preventing premature commitment to noisy evidence. Experiments on LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, and PersonaMem-v2 show consistent state-of-the-art accuracy; our new DiMoBench confirms that PreCoMem adapts to non-stationary belief drift without over-reacting to noise. The code is available at https://github.com/ZhongWishing/PreCoMem.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Harness Scaling for Generalizable Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29458v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent harnesses have become a central component of modern agentic systems, shaping how models use tools, manage context, and coordinate long-horizon interaction. However, existing approaches typically train through a single fixed harness, which can lead to specialization and limited transfer across harness configurations. To address this, we introduce \textbf{Harness Scaling}, a new scaling dimension for agentic RL that extends training from a single harness to diverse execution configurations. Concretely, we construct a factorized harness space, select a compact training subset with balanced factorial coverage, and organize cross-harness training to combine global coverage with controlled variation for the same task. Across multiple benchmarks, model scales, and RL algorithms, Harness Scaling consistently improves generalization to unseen white-box configurations. Moreover, models trained through structured white-box variation transfer effectively to unseen external black-box harnesses, outperforming models trained through a single complex black-box harness. These results establish harness diversity as a meaningful scaling dimension for agentic RL and highlight the importance of how this diversity is organized for cross-harness generalization.</p></details> |  |

Thus, Rego policy repair remains a manual process driven by trial and error. To address this, we introduce RegoBench, the first automated Rego policy benchmark comprising 1,159 real-world defects, and propose RegoMender, a feedback-guided multi-agent framework. Collaborating via a shared blackboard and an atomic toolkit, RegoMender dynamically executes patching, rewriting, or rolling back, to resolve complex repair scenarios. Evaluated on RegoBench, RegoMender achieves syntactic and test-based semantic success rates of 82.9\% and 77.9\%, respectively, outperforming the strongest baseline by 13.4\%. Unlike other approaches whose performance drops in complex scenarios, RegoMender maintains effectiveness across single-step and chained repair tasks. Furthermore, it generalizes strongly across HCL and SQL and achieves an 86.4\% repair rate on 132 real-world CVE vulnerabilities, proving its robustness and practical utility for security.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 5 figures, accepted to ASE 2026, research Track</p></details> |
| **[SUDP: Secret-Use Delegation Protocol for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24920v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems now act with user secrets for APIs, messaging platforms, and cloud services. Today's agent runtimes typically implement authorization by exposure: enabling action means placing a reusable secret, or a reusable artifact derived from it, inside the runtime, so a transient prompt-injection or tool-side compromise becomes durable account compromise. Existing defenses cover adjacent pieces such as secret storage, scoped delegation, sender-constrained tokens, and runtime monitoring, but none states the combined requirement: an untrusted autonomous requester should be able to cause a user-authorized secret-backed operation without gaining reusable authority over it. We formalize this as the Agent Secret Use (ASU) problem and identify seven security properties any solution must satisfy, spanning authorization integrity and secret confidentiality. We propose the Secret-Use Delegation Protocol (SUDP), in which a requester proposes a canonical operation, the user authorizes it with a fresh authenticator-backed grant, and a custodian redeems the grant to perform the bounded use; reusable authority never crosses the requester boundary. We specialize SUDP for LLM-driven agents, where it applies whenever a tool call would exercise user-enrolled authority-bearing material. Under standard cryptographic assumptions, SUDP satisfies six of the seven properties; the seventh, runtime-memory confidentiality, is closed by composition with a hardware-rooted runtime. A reference implementation, available at https://github.com/xhyumiracle/sudp, adds sub-millisecond protocol overhead per mediated call.</p></details> |  |
| **[CTBench: Evaluating Troubleshooting Capabilities of AI Agents in Realistic Telecom Network Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12002v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While autonomous agents show promise for network operations, existing telecom troubleshooting evaluations fail to capture the partial observability and heterogeneity of real-world telecom environments. We introduce CTBench, a public benchmark evaluating agent troubleshooting capabilities in root cause analysis (RCA) and path restoration. Our evaluation of state-of-the-art agents reveals that while they excel at path endpoint identification, they severely underperform in RCA. Crucially, even when agents produce correct answers, they frequently fail to gather the requisite diagnostic evidence demanded in operational practice. Finally, we demonstrate that increased computational expenditure does not guarantee improved diagnostic competence, highlighting critical gaps in current agent capabilities.</p></details> |  |
| **[One Act, Many Masks: Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29427v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) have substantially improved the performance of LLM agents on complex, long-horizon interactive tasks. However, despite progress in fine-grained credit assignment, policy optimization still applies these learning signals through response-specific probability ratios. Different textual responses can induce the same executable effect, yet credit differences arising from subsequent outcomes remain tied to their individual textual realizations. We identify this mismatch as credit-update misalignment. Our analysis reveals that credit-text pairing directly shapes policy-update directions, even when the policy context and executable effect are held fixed. Motivated by this finding, we propose Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization (EMPO), which aligns policy optimization with execution-level equivalence through an effect-level surrogate that couples updates across alternative textual realizations. EMPO preserves the original credit signals, integrates with both trajectory-level and step-level credit estimators, and requires no additional critics or rollouts. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Sokoban show that EMPO outperforms strong baselines in both task performance and learning efficiency.</p></details> | 20 pages, 11 figures |
| **[BIRD-RL: Scaling Agentic Reinforcement Learning over Stateful Data-Centric Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29426v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While agentic reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for training small language models (SLMs), existing frameworks are predominantly tool-centric and struggle with data-centric environments. Specifically, applying tool-centric frameworks to stateful tasks like SQL debugging introduces severe spatial inefficiencies since proper environment isolation typically requires a separate database container per rollout. Naively reusing databases without tracking evolving states across multi-turn interactions causes trajectory contamination and cross-trajectory interference, which severely destabilizes policy updates. To overcome these bottlenecks, we introduce BIRD-RL, a novel four-stage agentic RL curriculum that alternately enhances foundational agent reasoning and trajectory-adaptive on-policy exploration to circumvent the instability inherently associated with direct end-to-end learning. Crucially, to support this curriculum, BIRD-RL is driven by a Trajectory-scoped Persistent Execution Infrastructure, which enables safe database replica reuse while preserving trajectory isolation. This design improves spatial RL efficiency by 12.8x, successfully enabling scalable agentic RL for complex data-centric tasks without needs of Kubernettes. Empirical evaluations on the BIRD-CRITIC-SQLite benchmark demonstrate that our 7B and 14B models achieve Success Rates of 44.40% and 48.00%, respectively, showing competitive performance against strong proprietary model-based agents such as Claude-Opus-4.6 Agent (48.20%). Furthermore, BIRD-RL successfully trains unified SLMs capable of both advanced SQL debugging and generation, highlighting its broader potential for multi-objective optimization across heterogeneous, real-world data-centric tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 Main Track</p></details> |
| **[Learning from Repair Experience: Self-Evolving Agents for Automated Vulnerability Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30105v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise for automated vulnerability repair (AVR), but they still face several limitations, including the lack of intra-vulnerability experience accumulation and the lack of cross-vulnerability experience reuse. To address these challenges, we propose EvoRepair, an experience-based self-evolving AVR agent framework that enables LLMs to accumulate, refine, and leverage domain-specific knowledge across long-horizon vulnerability repairs. EvoRepair follows a cyclic learn-and-repair process that retrieves relevant past experiences to guide repair, extracts new experiences from repair trajectories, and updates an experience bank using quality-aware scoring. We evaluate EvoRepair against 15 representative vulnerability repair baselines on PATCHEVAL and SEC-bench. Results show that EvoRepair achieves the best overall performance, reaching 93.47% on PATCHEVAL, 87.00% on SEC-bench, and 90.46% overall. In particular, EvoRepair outperforms the latest LLM-based baseline LoopRepair by 39.56% and 33.50% on PATCHEVAL and SEC-bench, respectively, and surpasses IntentFix by 70.86% and 50.50%. Across both benchmarks, EvoRepair also exceeds the recent self-evolving agent Live-SWE-Agent by 6.98% overall. Additional transfer experiments on VUL4J further demonstrate the robustness of EvoRepair across models, programming languages, and datasets. These findings demonstrate that experience-based self-evolution substantially strengthens agentic AVR and goes beyond existing self-evolving techniques.</p></details> | <details><summary>has b...</summary><p>has been accepted to ICSE 2027</p></details> |
| **[LiveAct: Towards Real-Time Multi-Agent World Models with Heterogeneous Action Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29408v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive video world models predict how an environment evolves under user actions, but most existing systems assume one controlled agent and one task-specific action interface. We study multi-agent video world modeling, where one model generates a joint visual future for multiple controlled agents while interpreting heterogeneous controls ranging from binary game buttons to continuous robot commands. This task presents two complementary challenges. First, every agent-indexed action stream must control its intended visual entity independently. Second, the model must adapt the meaning of each control variable across different tasks, where the same tensor dimension may denote an attack, steering command, or robot command. We introduce LiveAct, centered on a Heterogeneous Action Interface (HAI) whose two components mirror this decomposition. To support independent control, Agent-Aware Spatial Modulation uses language-driven spatial gates and agent-specific attention to bind each action stream to its intended visual agent. To accommodate heterogeneous controls, Schema-Guided Action Adaptation describes each control variable in language and modulates its semantic token with the synchronized numerical value. We further transfer the complete interface to a blockwise causal generator through teacher forcing and rollout-aware distribution matching, enabling efficient streaming generation while retaining agent-specific control and action semantics. We instantiate the formulation on six multi-agent sources spanning games and multi-robot manipulation, and evaluate visual quality, action following, agent attribution, and latency. Across games and robot manipulation, LiveAct reaches 0.898 and 0.942 agent-attribution accuracy without agent locations or identity crops, and the causal generator attains 17.70 FPS at $512\times 448$.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://liveact-ai.github.io</p></details> |
| **[2-Step Agent: How a Bayesian Decision Maker Learns from AI-Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21889v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prognostic predictions from ML models support human decision making in several fields, including high-stakes ones such as healthcare and the judiciary. Yet, we still lack a clear understanding of how decision makers learn from ML-based decision support (ML-DS). In this paper, we introduce a general computational framework, the 2-Step Agent, to capture how a prediction from an ML model can also be used as a source of useful information about the training data and the causal mechanism that generated it. Our framework models (i) how a prediction from an ML model affects the beliefs of a rational Bayesian agent, and (ii) how this change in beliefs affects the estimation of the causal effect, the downstream decision for a new observation, and the subsequent outcome. In addition to the framework itself, we make three contributions. First, for the multivariate linear-Gaussian setting, we derive a tractable solution for the Bayesian inference problem introduced, i.e. one in which the agent infers information about a population from an ML prediction. Second, we experimentally identify conditions under which ML-DS is beneficial compared to unassisted decision making. Third, we show that a single misaligned prior belief of the user can be sufficient for ML-DS to lead to worse downstream outcomes compared to no decision support even when the prognostic model is well-specified and the agent is perfectly rational. Hence, even under ideal conditions, ML-DS can do more harm than good.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Camera-ready version. 39 pages, 8 figures</p></details> |
| **[Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29357v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous agents powered by foundation models can reason, plan, and use tools, but their fixed weights and context limits hinder sustained learning from experience over long-term deployment. While external memory systems attempt to bridge this gap, they mostly act as passive retrieval caches, evaluated primarily by whether they can recall stored facts or past interactions. Whether memory can drive genuine continual learning remains an open question. In this paper, we introduce Memory-Centric Continual Learning (MCCL), arguing that memory should be judged by whether it improves future actions, not merely what it retrieves. MCCL has three main parts. First, a three-axis taxonomy organizes memory by where state is stored (substrates), what form it takes (assets), and how it is managed (policies). This defines a two-loop lifecycle that updates memory state in an inner loop while refining management policies in an outer loop based on task feedback. Second, an evaluation protocol measures retention under interference, adaptation to change, and task transfer to isolate genuine behavioral gains from simple context expansion. Third, a memory scaling framework models how agent performance scales with experience, storage capacity, and compute budgets across non-parametric and parametric memory. Together, these components reframe agent memory from passive lookup to continuous learning, offering a principled route toward self-improving autonomous agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scaling Verifiable Environments for Long-horizon Work Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04906v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Work agents operate over digital artifacts to execute professional knowledge-intensive work, requiring training environments that support long-horizon interaction and trustworthy verification. To bridge this gap, we introduce WorkForge, a scalable synthesis framework for constructing verifiable work-agent environments from real-world resources. Starting from expert workflows, WorkForge first identifies the resources, decisions, and deliverables required by each workflow. It then retrieves relevant real-world files and organizes them into a workspace. WorkForge inspects the workspace to extract concrete, checkable facts about its content. These factual anchors fix which task types the workspace can support and how their outcomes can be verified. Therefore, WorkForge derives each task's instructions, solution plan, and complementary programmatic and semantic verifiers directly from these factual anchors, keeping verification traceable to observable workspace evidence. Furthermore, we construct 16.7K verifiable environments across 40 professional domains, with workspaces collectively covering 60 file types. Post-training Qwen3.5-35B-A3B-Base improves GDPVal from 45.5 to 73.6 and APEX Score from 5.0 to 21.3, while enabling Qwen3.5-27B to achieve highly competitive performance and outperform strong competitors. | **[Benchmarking and Error Analysis of Deep Search and Deep Research LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29346v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve into autonomous Deep Search and Deep Research Agents capable of executing long-horizon, multi-step tasks, evaluating their reliability becomes increasingly critical. However, existing evaluation paradigms predominantly focus on final task outcomes, leaving a significant gap in understanding the intermediate execution traces, where agents are highly susceptible to various errors. To address this critical gap, we introduce a comprehensive methodology and analytical framework designed specifically to diagnose errors in intermediate workflows. Building upon this framework, we establish a structured taxonomy that categorizes these vulnerabilities into two primary dimensions: System-Level Errors and Task-Level Errors. Through a systematic examination of step-by-step execution traces and agent-environment interactions, we reveal how these underlying errors affect overall performance. Finally, through empirical experiments and systematic analysis of the identified error types, we provide actionable design guidelines, aiming to enhance the robustness, reasoning capabilities, and reliability of Deep Search and Deep Research agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proce...</summary><p>Proceedings of the 1st ICML Workshop on Failure Modes in Agentic AI (FAGEN), Seoul, South Korea, 2026</p></details> |
| **[Workflow-Aware Communication and Computation Scheduling for Mobile Edge AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29337v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly deployed in mobile edge environments to autonomously handle diverse tasks through multi-step workflows. Unlike conventional dependency-aware tasks with predefined dependency structures, agent workflows are dynamically generated based on intermediate execution results. To minimize the weighted sum of job completion latencies, this paper models partially revealed agent workflows using dynamic workflow graphs and formulates an online scheduling problem that jointly optimizes computation placement, wireless resource block allocation, and edge computing resource allocation. To address the uncertainty of future workflow evolution, we first transform the weighted completion time objective into an equivalent per-slot holding cost and characterize the optimal policy through a Bellman equation. We then construct a tractable look-ahead cost-to-go surrogate based on the revealed workflow and the estimated remaining latencies of active jobs. The proposed scheduling algorithm leverages the surrogate to determine computation placement and uses the resulting operation criticality to allocate wireless and edge computing resources online. Simulation results show that the proposed algorithm outperforms the benchmark schemes across diverse scenarios.</p></details> |  |
| **[Do Coding Agents Understand Why Systems Crash---and Why They Don't?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29332v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents are increasingly capable of fixing real-world bugs, yet they still struggle with large systems codebases such as the Linux kernel. To understand why, we test how agents reason about execution in these codebases through crash reasoning: predicting from source code whether a given input crashes the system. A correct prediction alone, however, does not establish this ability, since it may come from merely spotting suspicious code. In this paper, we propose source interventions: we pair source trees that differ by a single commit, so that the same input crashes only one of them, and test whether agents predict crashes more often on the crashing tree. Experiments with four coding agents on SysRB, our dataset of 934 such pairs from Linux and OSS-Fuzz projects, yield three main findings. First, agents are biased toward predicting no crash: they rarely raise false alarms but miss many real crashes, and even the best agent misses over a quarter of them. Second, locating the code that determines the crash outcome is not enough: agents often misjudge its effect, especially when the crash occurs far from that code. Third, testing only on crashing code is misleading: agents that merely notice suspicious code predict crashes on both source trees, which would look like correct reasoning on the crashing tree alone. Together, these findings indicate that current coding agents cannot yet reliably reason about execution in complex systems codebases, and that paired evaluation is necessary to measure this ability.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentMonopoly: Behavioral Patterns and Persona Control in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29330v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How do LLM agents' strategies differ, and how much can instructions to adopt a persona change their behavior over a game? We study these questions with AgentMonopoly, an environment where agents communicate, buy and trade properties, and make decisions that affect later turns. We compare models from four families, each at two sizes, in games with identical models, different sizes of the same family, or different families. We also assign one agent a persona, a description of how it should behave, intended to change a tendency observed in games without that persona. The tested models differ in how often they trade, waive rent, and manage cash and properties. Larger models do not consistently win more games. Persona instructions change the words agents use and some of their actions, but the changes do not always match the role across all decisions. These results show why persona following should be assessed through actions and outcomes as well as language. A planning agent then uses retrieved examples to choose and order audio effects, such as volume control and reverb, for tracks, subgroups, and the full mix. To refine the mix, a detector identifies what needs correction, and a critic proposes numerical parameter adjustments from tracks to the full mix. In our experiments, prompting recent multimodal models to listen and revise often worsens the mix, making reliable correction a central challenge. We therefore build MixCritic, a dataset of 45,657 repair critiques derived from parameters fitted to professional mixes, and train the critic with supervised fine-tuning followed by preference optimization on rendered audio outcomes. On MedleyDB, learned refinement reduces error by 19% relative to the initial mix, with preference optimization reducing it by 8.4% over supervised training alone. Retrieved examples further improve effects selection, and a human listening test shows that MixAgent gets higher quality ratings than mixes produced by Qwen3-Omni, Gemini 3.8 Flash and GPT-4o-audio.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Atomic to Agentic: Towards Interpretable Evaluation of LLMs' Agentic Mathematical Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26950v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving from performing end-to-end mathematical reasoning to integrating agentic intelligence. However, most existing math benchmarks evaluate only final answers. This outcome-oriented evaluation provides limited diagnostic value for identifying process-level failures or rigorous logic, failing to guide the transformation of LLMs into robust agents. To bridge this gap, we present a process-level benchmark designed to evaluate the inherent agentic mathematical reasoning abilities of LLMs. Our framework aligns problem-solving agentic behaviors with a structured taxonomy of reusable mathematical atomic capabilities. We design a comprehensive suite of planning, action, and feedback tasks across both textual and multimodal contexts, supported by an automated pipeline that synthesizes high-quality trajectories and produces fine-grained annotations via controlled LLM rewriting. Experiments reveal that models with similar end-to-end accuracy can exhibit markedly different agentic capability profiles. This demonstrates that process-level evaluation is crucial for interpreting the true potential of LLMs and guiding the development of next-generation mathematical agents.</p></details> | EMNLP 2026 |
| **[Long-MDR: Long-Context Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Deep-Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05195v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The next generation of multimodal research agents must reason over long-lived research histories rather than short model completions. During a single task, an agent may repeatedly search the web, inspect visual evidence, revisit earlier hypotheses, and accumulate tens of thousands of tokens of multimodal context. Despite this trend, online RL for multimodal research agents remains largely confined to shorter contexts and interaction horizons. We push online RL training to 128k context and 75+ tool-interaction turns. To our knowledge, this is the first online multimodal deep-research RL study trained at 128k context, and the first trained with a 75 tool-turn horizon. Scaling to this regime exposes several practical limitations of conventional RL training. Early in training, weak policies make poor use of large interaction budgets, causing expensive rollouts with little reward improvement. Later, policy entropy can collapse before performance has saturated, prematurely ending useful learning. We introduce Long-MDR, a three-component training recipe designed specifically for this setting: On-Policy Distillation Warmup, Progressive Horizon Expansion, and Entropy-Triggered Rescue. Together, these techniques improve both the learning efficiency and stability of long-horizon RL, enabling continued gains in a regime where direct training is slow and costly. Code will be available at https://github.com/navileon/Long-MDR</p></details> |  |
| **[Forge: An Agentic System for ERGM Specification and Interpretation in Social Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04306v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exponential Random Graph Models (ERGMs) provide a statistical framework for testing hypotheses about how ties form using network structure and node attributes. Their usefulness depends on model specification, which often requires researchers to repeatedly propose models, fit them, check how well they reproduce the observed network, and revise them when they do not. We present Forge (Formation-Oriented Reasoning with Guarded ERGMs), an agentic system that performs this process using a language model and external statistical tools. Given an observed network and a domain description, Forge proposes hypotheses about tie formation, translates them into candidate ERGM specifications, evaluates them statistically, refines the best candidate using goodness-of-fit feedback, and returns a human-readable interpretation of the final model. On 16 benchmark networks, Forge improves on the best baseline on 13 networks and matches it on the other three, with a median 24.8% improvement in goodness of fit. On 60 controlled networks with known generating specifications, Forge achieves exact specification recovery in 78.3% of cases and attains 90.8% term-level F1. These results show that Forge can reduce the manual trial-and-error involved in ERGM specification while making the process more reproducible and keeping model selection grounded in statistical evidence.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 4 figures. Substantially revised manuscript with updated framing, expanded experiments on 16 benchmark networks, and a controlled recovery study on 60 networks</p></details> |
| **[Resonance and Depth: Composing Timbral Identity and Spatial Form in an Artist-in-the-Loop Co-Creative Multi-Agent Percussive Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29026v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This research-creation artistic report presents Resonance and Depth, a 9-minute immersive electroacoustic/acousmatic performance that treats timbre and space as primary compositional materials. Built from live electronic percussion and a personal corpus recorded, curated, and composed by the author, the work investigates how timbral identity and musical form can emerge through real-time recombination, spatialisation, and performer-system interaction rather than conventional harmonic development. The performance is realised as an artist-in-the-loop co-creative multi-agent system integrating MACAT, MACataRT, a MIDI-based musical agent, a spatialisation layer, and a conductor agent. These components recombine and transform the performer's material through concatenative synthesis, audio mosaicing, symbolic mediation, physically inspired synthesis, and object-based spatialisation. The work approaches spatialisation as an active compositional process in which position, trajectory, depth, diffusion, width, and apparent size participate in musical phrasing and form. Through this practice-based framework, Resonance and Depth examines how performer identity can be retained, transformed, and redistributed across human, algorithmic, timbral, and spatial processes within an immersive co-creative performance ecology.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 1 figure. Proceedings of Timbre 2026, 4th International Conference on Timbre, Montréal, Québec, Canada. Research-creation artistic report</p></details> |
| **[HybridResearcher: Bridging the Structured-Unstructured Boundary for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24899v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While LLM agents are increasingly capable of gathering and synthesizing evidence from a single homogeneous source, such as structured tables or unstructured data, real-world analytical questions frequently span heterogeneous sources. For example, answering one enterprise question may require both a result computed over an internal database and evidence found on the web. Recent evaluations on benchmarks like HybridDeepResearch show that even frontier agents struggle when evidence must cross this boundary. While these benchmarks have exposed the limitation, methodologies to explicitly teach cross-source reasoning remain absent. To address this, we introduce HybridResearcher, a 4B agent trained to bridge evidence across the structured and unstructured boundary. To build it, we synthesize cross-source bridge trajectories by starting with validated single-source tasks-such as existing text-to-SQL or web search queries-and fabricating the missing source around a bridge entity drawn from the seed's own output. We retain a sample only when strict construction gates confirm that neither side alone suffices, ensuring the data requires true cross-source interdependence. Finally, we train on this curated dataset using supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning. Evaluated on the existing HybridDeepResearch benchmark, HybridResearcher improves over our base model, Qwen3.5-4B, by 30.0 points pass@8 and surpasses the far larger Qwen3.5-397B-A17B by 8.3 points, while also improving over its base model on established, held-out single-source benchmarks (BrowseComp and BrowseComp-Plus), demonstrating that this complex cross-source reasoning can be taught directly to small models without triggering catastrophic forgetting.</p></details> |  |
| **[GenXR-Bench: Benchmarking Coding Agents for Interactive XR Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29014v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents are rapidly advancing from single-file generation to long-horizon repository-scale engineering, yet the evaluation of their capabilities to author interactive extended reality (XR) applications remains an open challenge. Unlike traditional software, XR applications operate within dynamic 3D physical environments where runtime robustness depends jointly on continuous user motion, spatial input, and environmental sensing. We introduce GenXR-Bench, the first benchmark for the end-to-end evaluation of coding agents in XR software development. GenXR-Bench contains 112 expert-authored tasks spanning 24 feature implementations, 21 bug repairs, and 67 application generation tasks. Held-out tests combine scripted and adaptive interactions with programmatic and visual assertions to evaluate core functionality and runtime behavior. GenXR-Bench's testing suite achieves 96.5% accuracy against blinded expert judgments of 124 candidate applications executed on consumer XR hardware. Evaluating 10 models on GenXR-Bench, we find that the strongest configuration achieves only 50.9% overall Pass@1. Failure analysis identifies recurring misunderstandings in asynchronous sensor lifecycles, physical grounding, and embodied interaction semantics. GenXR-Bench establishes a reproducible testbed for evaluating coding agents in the interactive and physically situated conditions of spatial computing.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, 10 figures. Code available at https://github.com/whuang37/genxr-bench</p></details> |
